Global Hilbert expansion for some non-relativistic kinetic equations Thanks: Acknowledgment. The research of Yuanjie Lei was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grants 11971187 and 12171176, the research of Shuangqian Liu was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grant 12325107, the research of Qinghua Xiao was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grants 11871469 and 12271506, the research of Huijiang Zhao was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China grants 11731008 and 12221001. This work was also partially supported by the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities.
Yuanjie Lei, Shuangqian Liu, Qinghua Xiao, Huijiang Zhao

Abstract
. The Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau (VML) system and the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann (VMB) system are fundamental models in dilute collisional plasmas. In this paper, we are concerned with the hydrodynamic limits of both the VML and the non-cutoff VMB systems in the entire space. Our primary objective is to rigorously prove that, within the framework of Hilbert expansion, the unique classical solution of the VML or non-cutoff VMB system converges globally over time to the smooth global solution of the Euler-Maxwell system as the Knudsen number approaches zero. The core of our analysis hinges on deriving novel interplay energy estimates for the solutions of these two systems, concerning both a local Maxwellian and a global Maxwellian, respectively. Our findings address a problem in the hydrodynamic limit for Landau-type equations and non-cutoff Boltzmann-type equations with a magnetic field. Furthermore, the approach developed in this paper can be seamlessly extended to assess the validity of the Hilbert expansion for other types of kinetic equations.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
76X05; 35Q35; 82C40; 82D10; 35Q83

1.
Introduction

The smooth transition from kinetic equations describing perfect gases or particles to hydrodynamics is closely linked to Hilbert’s sixth problem. Hilbert himself introduced the renowned Hilbert expansion as the inaugural example of this program. In this expansion, the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
plays a central role, as detailed in references such as
[
38
,
66
]
. Rigorously substantiating the legitimacy of this expansion for kinetic equations has remained a challenging problem since its inception.

In this article, at the kinetic level, we investigate the rescaled Vlasov-Maxwell-Landau (VML) and Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann (VMB) systems. These systems represent the fundamental models governing the dynamics of dilute charged particles,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}F^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{\varepsilon}-e_{-}\Big(E^{\epsilon}+v\times B^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{C}(F^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon}),\\
&\partial_{t}E^{\varepsilon}-c\nabla_{x}\times B^{\varepsilon}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF^{\varepsilon}dv,\\
&\partial_{t}B^{\varepsilon}+c\nabla_{x}\times E^{\varepsilon}=0,\\
&\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{\varepsilon}=4\pi e_{-}\Big(1-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F^{\varepsilon}dv\Big),\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{\varepsilon}=0.\end{aligned}

(1.1)

Here,
\varepsilon>0
represents the Knudsen number, which characterizes the ratio of the mean free path to the characteristic length. The operators
\nabla_{x}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})
and
\nabla_{v}=(\partial_{v_{1}},\partial_{v_{2}},\partial_{v_{3}})
denote spatial and velocity gradients, respectively. The function
F^{\varepsilon}=F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\geq 0
represents the number density function for electrons at time
t\geq 0
, position
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and velocity
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In this context,
-e_{-}
denotes the charge of the electrons, and
c
stands for the speed of light. Furthermore, we normalize both constants
e_{-}
and
c
to one for simplicity in the rest of this paper.

Regarding the bilinear collision operator
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\cdot)
in equation (
1.1
), it can be associated with either the Landau collision operator or the Boltzmann collision operator:

•

For the Landau collision operator, denoted as
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\cdot)
, it is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(G,H)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{v}\cdot\left\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi(v-v^{\prime})\left[G(v^{\prime})\nabla_{v}H(v)-H(v)\nabla_{v^{\prime}}G(v^{\prime})\right]dv^{\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{3}\partial_{v_{i}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{ij}(v-v^{\prime})\left[G(v^{\prime})\partial_{v_{j}}H(v)-H(v)\partial_{v^{\prime}_{j}}G(v^{\prime})\right]dv^{\prime},

where
\phi(v)=\left(\phi^{ij}(v)\right)_{3\times 3}
is a non-negative matrix with components
\phi^{ij}(v)
given by

\phi^{ij}(v)=\left\{\delta_{ij}-\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{|v|^{2}}\right\}|v|^{\gamma+2},\qquad\gamma\geq-3.

(1.2)

Here,
\delta_{ij}
represents the Kronecker delta. We are particularly interested in the case where
\gamma=-3
, which corresponds to the original Landau collision operator with Coulombic interactions.

•

For the Boltzmann collision operator, also denoted as
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\cdot)
, it is defined as follows:

\mathcal{C}(G_{1},G_{2})(v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{\ast},\sigma)\left\{G_{1}(v_{\ast}^{\prime})G_{2}(v^{\prime})-G_{1}(v_{\ast})G_{2}(v)\right\}dv_{\ast}d\sigma.

Here,
(v,v_{\ast})
and
(v^{\prime},v_{\ast}^{\prime})
represent the pre-post collision velocities of the particles, and they satisfy

\displaystyle v^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{\ast}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{\ast}|}{2}\sigma,\ v_{\ast}^{\prime}=\frac{v+v_{\ast}}{2}-\frac{|v-v_{\ast}|}{2}\sigma.

The collision kernel
B(v-v_{\ast},\sigma)
is a non-negative function of the relative velocity
|v-v_{\ast}|
and the deviation angle
\theta
:

B(v-v_{\ast},\sigma)=C_{v}|v-v_{\ast}|^{\gamma}b(\cos\theta),\qquad\gamma>-3,

where
C_{v}>0
, and
\cos\theta=[(v-v_{\ast})\cdot\sigma]/|v-v_{\ast}|
. It’s worth noting that through standard symmetrization techniques, we can assume that
B(v-v_{\ast},\sigma)
is supported on the set
0\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
with
\cos\theta\geq 0
.
For the cutoff case, the function
b(\cos\theta)
satisfies the following inequality:

0\leq b(\cos\theta)\leq C|\cos\theta|,\qquad C>0.

In contrast, for the non-cutoff case,
b(\cos\theta)
obeys the following bounds:

\frac{1}{C_{b}\theta^{1+2s}}\leq\sin\theta b(\cos\theta)\leq\frac{C_{b}}{\theta^{1+2s}},\qquad\forall s\in(0,1),\quad\theta\in\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right],\quad C_{b}>1.

In this paper, our focus is specifically on the non-cutoff case, where
\gamma
lies within the range:

\max\Big\{-3,-\frac{3}{2}-2s\Big\}<\gamma<0.

The objective of this paper is to provide a rigorous justification for the global validity of the Hilbert expansion in both the VML and the non-cutoff VMB systems, as given in equation (
1.1
), within the entire space. This justification is carried out under the initial data conditions:

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=F_{0}^{\varepsilon}(x,v),\ E^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=E^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),\ B^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x).

(1.3)

1.1.
Hilbert expansions

In this subsection, motivated by Caflisch’s groundbreaking work
[
6
]
, we outline the standard Hilbert expansion procedures for the system (
1.1
).

1.1.1.
Hilbert expansion of the system (
1.1
)

Let
k\geq 2
, by plugging the Hilbert expansion

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad E^{\varepsilon}=E+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad B^{\varepsilon}=B+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R}\end{aligned}

(1.4)

into (
1.1
),
we then deduce that the coefficients
[F_{n}(t,x,v),

E_{n}(t,x),B_{n}(t,x)]

(0\leq n\leq 2k-1)
and the remainder
[F_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v),E_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x),

B_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x)]

satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}:

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{0})=0,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{0}:\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}&\partial_{t}F_{0}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}=\mathcal{C}(F_{1},F_{0})+\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{1}),\\
&\partial_{t}E-\nabla_{x}\times B=4\pi\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}vF_{0}dv,\\
&\partial_{t}B+\nabla_{x}\times E=0,\\
&\nabla_{x}\cdot E=4\pi\Big(1-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}F_{0}dv\Big),\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B=0,\end{array}\right.

\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{n}:

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}F_{n}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{n}-\Big(E_{n}+v\times B_{n}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{p}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n+1\\
i,j\geq 0\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j},

(1.9)

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}E_{n}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{n}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{n}dv,

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}B_{n}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{n}=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{n}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\hskip 130.88284pt\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2k-1}:

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}F_{2k-1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{2k-1}-\Big(E_{2k-1}+v\times B_{2k-1}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{2k-1}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=2k\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=2k-1\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j},

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}E_{2k-1}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{2k-1}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{2k-1}dv,

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}B_{2k-1}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{2k-1}=0,

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{2k-1}=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{2k-1}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{2k-1}=0,

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}[\mathcal{C}(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{0})+\mathcal{C}(F_{0},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})]

\displaystyle\quad=\varepsilon^{k-1}\mathcal{C}(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n-1}[\mathcal{C}(F_{i},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{C}(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{i})]+\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}\Big[\Big(E_{n}+v\times B_{n}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i}\Big]+\varepsilon^{k}\mathcal{Q},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{R}^{\varepsilon}dv,

(1.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=-4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0.

Here

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{Q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 2k+1\\
2\leq i,j\leq 2k-1\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{i+j-2k-1}Q(F_{i},F_{j})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\geq 2k\\
1\leq i,j\leq 2k-1\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{i+j-2k}\Big(E_{i}+v\times B_{i}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j}.\end{aligned}

1.1.2.
Determination of the coefficients

Let’s briefly outline how to determine the coefficients
[F_{0}(t,x,v),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
and
[F_{n}(t,x,v),E_{n}(t,x),B_{n}(t,x)]
for
1\leq n\leq 2k-1
.

Starting with (
1.1.1
)
1
, we can deduce that the zeroth-order coefficient is given by

F_{0}(t,x,v)=\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{[\rho,u,T]}=\frac{\rho(t,x)}{(2\pi RT(t,x))^{3/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|v-u(t,x)|^{2}}{2RT(t,x)}\right),

where
\rho(t,x),u(t,x)
, and
T(t,x)
represent the fluid density, bulk velocity, and temperature, respectively. Furthermore,
[\rho,u,T,E,B]
must satisfy the following compressible Euler-Maxwell system:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla_{x}\cdot(\rho u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}[\rho u]+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\rho u\otimes u\right)+\nabla_{x}(R\rho T)+\rho\big(E+u\times B\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Big[\rho\Big(e+\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\Big)\Big]+\nabla_{x}\cdot\Big[\rho u\Big(e+\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\Big)\Big]+\nabla_{x}\big(R\rho Tu\big)+\rho u\cdot E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E-\nabla_{x}\times B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi\rho u,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B+\nabla_{x}\times E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi\big(1-\rho\big),

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Here the pressure
p
and the internal energy
e
satisfy the following constitutive relations take the form:

p=R\rho T,\qquad e=\frac{3}{2}RT

with
R>0
being the gas constant. Without loss of generality, we can assume
R=\frac{2}{3}
.

Given the initial datum

\displaystyle[\rho,u,T,E,B](0,x)=[\rho_{0},u_{0},T_{0},E_{0},B_{0}](x),\ T_{0}=\rho_{0}^{2/3},

(1.12)

it is shown in
[
44
, Theorem 1.3, p. 2359]
that the Cauchy problem (
1.1.2
) and (
1.12
) does admit a global smooth solution
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
in the whole space.

Proposition 1.1

(Global solvability of the Euler-Maxwell system)
.

Let
N_{0}>10^{4}
be a sufficiently large integer and denote

\left\|\left[\rho_{0}-1,u_{0},T_{0}-1,E_{0},B_{0}\right]\right\|_{H^{N_{0}+1}}+\left\|(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)^{1/2}u_{0}\right\|_{Z}+\left\|(\mathbf{I}-\Delta)^{1/2}E_{0}\right\|_{Z}=\epsilon_{1},

where the norm
\|\cdot\|_{Z}
is defined by
[
44
, (2.18), p. 2371]
.

Assume that

\rho_{0}=-\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{0},\ B_{0}=\nabla_{x}\times u_{0},

and
\epsilon_{1}>0
is chosen suitably small, there exists a unique global smooth solution
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),

T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
with
T(t,x)=\rho(t,x)^{2/3}
to the Cauchy problem (
1.1.2
) and (
1.12
) which satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\infty]}\left\|\left[\rho(t,x)-1,T(t,x)-1,u(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)\right]\right\|_{H^{N_{0}+1}}

(1.13)

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,\infty]}\left[(1+t)^{p_{0}}\sup_{i\leq N_{1}}\left(\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}[\rho(t,x)-1]\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}[T(t,x)-1]\right\|_{\infty}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,\infty]}\Big[(1+t)^{p_{0}}\sup_{i\leq N_{1}+1}\left\|\left[\nabla_{x}^{i}u(t,x),\nabla_{x}^{i}E(t,x),\nabla_{x}^{i}B(t,x)\right]\right\|_{\infty}\Big]\leq C\epsilon_{1},

where
3\leq N_{1}\ll N_{0}
,
p_{0}>1
,
C
is some positive constant independent of
\epsilon_{1}
.

Once
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),

T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
is constructed as in Proposition
1.1
, we can then determine
[F_{1}(t,x,v),

E_{1}(t,x),B_{1}(t,x)]
as follows. Firstly, by setting
F_{1}=\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{1}
and using the definition (
1.25
), we write

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}[f_{1}]=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{1}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\chi_{0}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{R\rho T}}u_{1}^{i}\chi_{i}+\frac{T_{1}}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\chi_{4}

and

\displaystyle F_{1}=

\displaystyle\Big[\frac{\rho_{1}}{\rho}\mathbf{M}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\frac{v^{i}-u^{i}}{R\rho T}u_{1}^{i}\mathbf{M}+\frac{T_{1}}{6\rho}\Big(\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{RT}-3\Big)\mathbf{M}\Big]+\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\bf I-P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f_{1}].

(1.14)

Then it is straightforward to see that the microscopic part of
f_{1}=\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{1}
can be determined by

\displaystyle({\bf I-P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f_{1}]=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}^{-1}\Big[-\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{t}F_{0}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{0}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}\Big],

according to
\eqref{expan2}_{1}
, where the linearized collision operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
is defined in (
1.18
).

To further derive the macroscopic part of
F_{1}
, which is given in (
1.14
), by taking velocity moments
1,v,|v|^{2}
of (
1.9
) with
n=1
, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{1}+\nabla_{x}\cdot(\rho_{1}u+u_{1})=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{1}+(u\cdot\nabla_{x})u_{1}+(u_{1}\cdot\nabla_{x})u+(\nabla_{x}\cdot u)u_{1}+\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{x}(3RT\rho_{1}+RTT_{1})-\frac{R\rho_{1}}{\rho}\nabla_{x}(\rho T)

\displaystyle+\rho\big[E_{1}+\big(u\times B_{1}\big)\big]+u_{1}\times B=-\nabla_{x}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Big[(v-u)\otimes(v-u)-\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{3}\mathbf{I}\Big]F_{1}dv,

\displaystyle RT\big[\partial_{t}T_{1}+u\cdot\nabla_{x}T_{1}+(\nabla_{x}\cdot u)T_{1}\big]+2RT\nabla_{x}\cdot u_{1}+3R\nabla_{x}T\cdot u_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{2RT}{\rho}\nabla_{x}\rho\cdot u_{n}-2\big(u\times B\big)\cdot u_{n}

(1.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2u^{i}\partial_{x_{j}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Big[(v^{i}-u^{i})(v^{j}-u^{j})-\delta_{ij}\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{3}\Big]F_{1}dv\Big]

\displaystyle-\nabla_{x}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(v-u)\big[|v-u|^{2}-5RT\big]F_{1}dv

\displaystyle-2\partial_{x_{j}}\Big[u^{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Big[(v^{i}-u^{i})(v^{j}-u^{j})-\delta_{ij}\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{3}\Big]F_{1}dv\Big],

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E_{1}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{1}=4\pi\big(u_{1}+\rho_{1}u\big),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B_{1}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{1}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{1}=-\rho_{1},\ \nabla_{x}\cdot B_{1}=0,

where
\mathbf{I}
stands for the unit
3\times 3
matrix. Fortunately, (
1.1.2
) is a linear symmetrizable hyperbolic system with respect to the variables

[\rho_{1},u_{1},T_{1},E_{1},B_{1}]
and its Cauchy problem can be solved appropriately, cf.
[
60
]
.

We can then proceed with the above procedure step by step and ultimately determine all the coefficients
[F_{n}(t,x,v),E_{n}(t,x),B_{n}(t,x)]
under suitable initial data
[\rho_{n},u_{n},T_{n},E_{n},B_{n}](0,x)
for
1\leq n\leq 2k-1
. Therefore, our main objective, in order to verify (
1.4
), is to solve the remainders
[F_{R}^{\varepsilon},E_{R}^{\varepsilon},B_{R}^{\varepsilon}]
.

1.2.
The remainders

In this subsection, our aim is to explore the procedure for solving the remainder equations (
1.10
).

In order to solve
F_{R}^{\varepsilon}
, it is standard to introduce
f^{\varepsilon}
as

F_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}.

Then we can rewrite (
1.10
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\big)}{RT}\cdot\big(v-u\big)\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\frac{v-u}{2RT}f^{\varepsilon}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}\Big[\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}\Big]\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f^{\varepsilon},f^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n-1}\Big[\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n},f^{\varepsilon})+\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f^{\varepsilon},\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n})\Big]+\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\frac{v-u}{2RT}f^{\varepsilon}

(1.16)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}\Big[\Big(E_{n}+v\times B_{n}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}+\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{v}F_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle-\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}\Big[\Big(E_{n}+v\times B_{n}\Big)\cdot\frac{\big(v-u\big)}{2RT}f^{\varepsilon}\Big]+\varepsilon^{k}\mathcal{Q}_{0},

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}dv,\\
&\partial_{t}B_{R}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,\\
&\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{R}^{\varepsilon}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}dv,\qquad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{R}^{\varepsilon}=0,\end{aligned}

(1.17)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{0}=\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]
is the linearized Landau/Boltzmann collision operator defined by

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big[\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f)+\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}\big],

(1.18)

and

\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g)
is the nonlinear Landau/Boltzmann collision operator given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f,g)=

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}g).

(1.19)

On the other hand, if we introduce the global Maxwellian
\mu

\mu=\frac{1}{(2\pi RT_{c})^{3/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2RT_{c}}\right)

(1.20)

with the constant temperature
T_{c}
satisfying
\frac{1}{2}\sup_{t,x}T(t,x)<T_{c}<\inf_{t,x}T(t,x)
and set

F_{R}^{\varepsilon}=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h^{\varepsilon},

then it is straightforward to see that
h^{\varepsilon}
satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\big)}{RT}\cdot\big(v-u\big)\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{M}

\displaystyle+\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}-\Big(E+v\times B\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}+\frac{\mathcal{L}[h^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}]}{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},h^{\varepsilon})+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n-1}[\Gamma(\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n},h^{\varepsilon})+\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n})]

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{k}\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{k}\frac{E_{R}^{\varepsilon}\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}\Big[\Big(E_{n}+v\times B_{n}\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}+\Big(E_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\times B_{R}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\cdot\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{v}F_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle-\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}\frac{E_{n}\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{k}\mathcal{Q}_{1}.

(1.21)

Here
\mathcal{Q}_{1}=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{L}[f]
,
and
\Gamma(f,g)
are defined as (
1.18
) and (
1.19
) respectively, with
\mathbf{M}
replaced by
\mu
.
In addition,
\mathcal{L}_{d}[f]
is a linear collision operator defined as

-\mathcal{L}_{d}[f]=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}-\mu,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f)+\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}f,\mathbf{M}-\mu)\right].

(1.22)

The primary objective of this paper is to establish specific estimates for both
f^{\varepsilon}
and
h^{\varepsilon}
, ensuring the global applicability of the Hilbert expansion for both the VML and the non-cutoff VMB systems (
1.1
) in the whole space.

1.3.
Notations

Throughout this paper,
C
denotes a generic positive constant which is independent of the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
but may change line by line. The notation
a\lesssim b
means that there exists a generic positive constant
C
independent of the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
such that
a\leq Cb
and
a\approx b
means that both
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
hold.

The multi-indices
\alpha=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}]
and
\beta=[\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}]
will be used to record spatial and velocity derivatives, respectively. And
\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{x_{1}}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x_{2}}\partial^{\alpha_{3}}_{x_{3}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{v_{1}}\partial^{\beta_{2}}_{v_{2}}\partial^{\beta_{3}}_{v_{3}}
. Similarly, the notation
\partial^{\alpha}
will be used when
\beta=0
and likewise for
\partial_{\beta}
. The length of
\alpha
is denoted by
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}
.
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
means that no component of
\alpha^{\prime}
is greater than the corresponding component of
\alpha
, and
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
means that
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
and
|\alpha^{\prime}|<|\alpha|
. And it is convenient to write
\nabla_{x}^{k}=\partial^{\alpha}
with
|\alpha|=k
.

(\cdot,\cdot)
is used to denote the
L^{2}
inner product in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
, with the norm
|\cdot|_{L^{2}}
. For notational simplicity,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the
{L^{2}}
inner product either in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
or in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}
with the norm
\|\cdot\|
. For each non-negative integer
k
and
1\leq p\leq+\infty
, we also use
W^{k,p}
to denote the standard Sobolev spaces for
(x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
or
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and denote
H^{s}=W^{s,2}
with

\|f\|^{2}_{H^{s}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=0}^{s}\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\|^{2}.
Recall that
\nabla_{x}=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})
,
\nabla_{v}=(\partial_{v_{1}},\partial_{v_{2}},\partial_{v_{3}})
.

We now introduce some norms for the VML system and the non-cutoff VMB system case by case.

1.3.1.
Norms for the VML system

Let

\sigma^{ij}(v)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{ij}(v-v^{\prime})\mu(v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}

with
\phi^{ij}(v)
defined by (
1.2
), we now introduce the dissipation norm in
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by

\displaystyle|f(t,x)|_{\bf D}^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)\partial_{v_{i}}f(t,x,v)\partial_{v_{j}}f(t,x,v)\,dv

(1.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4R^{2}T^{2}_{c}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)v_{i}v_{j}|f(t,x,v)|^{2}\,dv,

the corresponding dissipation norm in
(x,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|_{\bf D}^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)\partial_{v_{i}}f(t,x,v)\partial_{v_{j}}f(t,x,v)\,dvdx

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4R^{2}T^{2}_{c}}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\sigma^{ij}(v)v_{i}v_{j}|f(t,x,v)|^{2}\,dvdx,

and the Sobolev dissipation norm

\displaystyle\|f(t)\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{\bf D}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{m}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f(t)\right\|^{2}_{\bf D},\qquad m\leq 2.

Here and in the sequel, Einstein’s summation convention is used.

For constant
\ell\geq m
, we set
\langle v\rangle=\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}
and introduce the following time-velocity dependent weight functions

\displaystyle w_{i}(t,v):=\langle v\rangle^{\ell-i}\exp\left(\frac{\langle v\rangle^{2}}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right),\quad 0\leq i\leq m

(1.24)

and then define the weighted norms

\displaystyle\|wf\|^{2}_{H^{m}}:=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}f\right\|^{2},\qquad\|wf\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{\bf D}}:=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}f\right\|^{2}_{\bf D}.

1.3.2.
Norms for the non-cutoff VMB system

For improved clarity, we will introduce separate notations for the non-cutoff VMB system.

As described in
[
1
,
12
]
, we introduce the following norms for the non-cutoff VMB system:

\begin{split}\left|f\right|^{2}_{\bf D}:=&\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{\ast},\sigma){\bf M}_{*}(f^{\prime}-f)^{2}dv_{\ast}dvd\sigma\\
&+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{6}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B(v-v_{\ast},\sigma)f_{*}^{2}\Big(\sqrt{{\bf M}_{*}^{\prime}}-\sqrt{{\bf M}_{*}}\Big)^{2}dvdv_{\ast}d\sigma,\\
\|f\|_{\bf D}^{2}:=&\left\||f|_{\bf D}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}},\end{split}

For
\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
L^{2}_{\ell}
represents the weighted space with the norm

|f|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\ell}}=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{\ell}|f(v)|^{2}dv,\quad\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\ell}}=\left\||f|_{L^{2}_{\ell}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

The weighted fractional Sobolev norm is given by

\left|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}f\right|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\gamma}}=\left|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}u\right|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\gamma}}+\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\frac{\left|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}f(v)-\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{\ell}f(v^{\prime})\right|^{2}}{|v-v^{\prime}|^{3+2s}}\chi_{|v-v^{\prime}|\leq 1}dv^{\prime}dv,

which is equivalent to

\left|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}f\right|^{2}_{H^{s}_{\gamma}}=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\left|\left(1-\triangle_{v}\right)^{\frac{s}{2}}\left(\langle v\rangle^{\ell}(v)f(v)\right)\right|^{2}dv.

We introduce the following time-velocity dependent weight function:

\displaystyle\overline{w}(t,v):=\exp\left(\frac{\langle v\rangle}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right),

and define the corresponding weighted norms:

\displaystyle\|\overline{w}h\|^{2}_{H^{m}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=0}^{m}\left\|\overline{w}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right\|^{2},\qquad\|\overline{w}h\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{\bf D}}:=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=0}^{m}\left\|\overline{w}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h\right\|^{2}_{\bf D}.

For simplicity, let’s also denote

\displaystyle X(t):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right),

it is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle Y(t):=-\frac{X^{\prime}(t)}{X(t)}=\frac{1}{8RT_{c}(\mathrm{e}+t)\big(\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)\big)^{2}}>0.

Next, it is straightforward to determine the null space of the linearized Landau/Boltzmann operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
,

\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{M}}=\mbox{span}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}},(v_{i}-u_{i})\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(1\leq i\leq 3),\left(\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{RT}-3\right)\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

Similarly, for the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
,

\mathcal{N}=\mbox{span}\left\{\mu^{\frac{1}{2}},v_{i}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(1\leq i\leq 3),(|v|^{2}-3)\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

Now, let’s define
{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}
as the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}_{v}
onto
\mathcal{N}_{\bf M}
and
{\bf P}
as the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}_{v}
onto
\mathcal{N}
. Additionally, we will use
\chi_{j}

(0\leq j\leq 4)
to denote the orthonormal basis of the null space of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
, given by

\displaystyle\chi_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\mathbf{M}},\quad\chi_{i}=\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{{\sqrt{R\rho T}}}\sqrt{\mathbf{M}},\ (i=1,2,3),\quad\chi_{4}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\left(\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{RT}-3\right)\sqrt{\mathbf{M}}.

Using these orthonormal basis functions, we can represent
{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}[f_{n}](1\leq n\leq 2k-1)
as

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}[f_{n}]=\frac{\rho_{n}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\chi_{0}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{R\rho T}}u_{n}^{i}\chi_{i}+\frac{T_{n}}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\chi_{4}.

(1.25)

Here,
[\rho_{n},u_{n},T_{n}]
are the hydrodynamic fields representing the density, velocity, and temperature fluctuations of
f_{n}
, respectively.

From Proposition
1.1
, we can easily deduce that

Corollary 1.1
.

For any given generic positive constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, if we assume further that
\epsilon_{1}
is sufficiently small, then one can always find a positive constant
T_{c}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{C_{0}}\leq T(t,x)-T_{c}\leq C_{0}\epsilon_{1}

(1.26)

holds for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

1.4.
Main results

In this subsection, we will present our main results concerning the global Hilbert expansion of the VML system and the non-cutoff VMB system. To start, we will first state the result for the VML system.

1.4.1.
Main result for the VML system

Setting
\kappa=\frac{1}{3}
, we define the energy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{2}\varepsilon^{i}\left[\left(\left\|\sqrt{4\pi RT}\nabla_{x}^{i}f^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}B_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right)+\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right],

(1.27)

and the dissipation rate

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)\backsimeq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2}\varepsilon^{i}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla_{x}^{i}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})\left[f^{\varepsilon}\right](t)\right\|^{2}_{\bf D}+\varepsilon^{\kappa}\left\|w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\bf D}\right.

(1.28)

\displaystyle\left.+\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}Y(t)\left\|(1+|v|)w_{i}\nabla_{x}^{i}h^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\right].

Our main result can now be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1

(Global Hilbert expansion of the VML system)
.

Assume that

•

The initial datum
[F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v),E^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)]
satisfies

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R,0}\geq 0,

and

\displaystyle E^{\varepsilon}_{0}=E_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R,0},\ B^{\varepsilon}_{0}=B_{0}+\sum_{nB=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R,0},

where
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
is the smooth solution constructed in Proposition
1.1
for the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.1.2
);

•

T_{c}
is suitably chosen such that there exists a positive constant
C_{0}>1
and (
1.26
) is valid for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

•

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for
N\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha_{0}+|\alpha|\leq N+4k-2n+2}

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial^{\alpha}[\rho_{n,0},u_{n,0},T_{n,0},E_{n,0},B_{n,0}](x)\right\|\leq C,\ \ 1\leq n\leq 2k-1;

(1.29)

•

Let
k\geq 3
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(0)\lesssim 1.

Then, there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, the Cauchy problem of the VML system (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) admits a unique smooth solution
[F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\varepsilon}(t,x),B^{\varepsilon}(t,x)]
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\geq 0,\quad E^{\varepsilon}=E+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad B^{\varepsilon}=B+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R}.\end{aligned}

Furthermore, for
\kappa=\frac{1}{3}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(s)\,ds\lesssim\mathcal{E}(0)+1,\ 0\leq t\leq\varepsilon^{-\kappa},

(1.30)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\varepsilon^{-\kappa}}\Big(\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t)\left(F^{\varepsilon}(t)-\mathbf{M}(t)\right)\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|E^{\varepsilon}(t)-E(t)\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|B^{\varepsilon}(t)-B(t)\right\|_{H^{2}}\Big)=0.

The following remark is concerned with the initial condition of the coefficients
F_{n}
(1\leq n\leq 2k-1)
in the Hilbert expansion.

Remark 1.1
.

The
t-
derivatives in (
1.29
) are implied by the moment equations from the evolution equations in (
1.1.1
). For example,
\partial_{t}[\rho_{n,0},u_{n,0},T_{n,0},E_{n,0},B_{n,0}]
can be defined through the evolution equation of
\rho_{n},u_{n},T_{n},E_{n},B_{n}
, as described in (
4.2
). It’s important to note that only the initial data of the macroscopic part of the coefficients are specified, as the microscopic component is determined iteratively according to the relations in (
1.29
).

1.4.2.
Main result for the non-cutoff VMB system

Our second result is concerned with the global Hilbert expansion of the non-cutoff VMB system.

In addition to the previous definitions, we also define the following energy functional:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{|\alpha|\leq 4}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|}\big(\|\sqrt{4\pi RT}\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+\|\partial^{\alpha}E_{R}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}+\|\partial^{\alpha}B^{\varepsilon}_{R}\|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4,|\beta|\geq 1}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+|\beta|}\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f^{\varepsilon}]\|^{2}+\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+|\beta|+1+\kappa}\|\overline{w}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2},

(1.31)

where
\kappa=\frac{1}{3}
, and the dissipation rate:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)\backsimeq

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+|\beta|-1}\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f^{\varepsilon}]\|^{2}_{\bf D}+Y(t)\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+|\beta|+\kappa}\big\|{\langle v\rangle}^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{w}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h^{\varepsilon}\big\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+|\beta|+\kappa}\|\overline{w}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}h^{\varepsilon}]\|^{2}_{\bf D}.

(1.32)

Our second main result can now be stated as follows

Theorem 1.2

(Global Hilbert expansion of the non-cutoff VMB system)
.

Assume that

\max\left\{-3,-\frac{3}{2}-2s\right\}<\gamma<0

for the non-cutoff collision kernel, and consider the following conditions:

•

The initial datum
[F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v),E^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)]
satisfies

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R,0}\geq 0,

and

\displaystyle E^{\varepsilon}_{0}=E_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R,0},\quad B^{\varepsilon}_{0}=B_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n,0}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R,0},

where
[\rho(t,x),u(t,x),T(t,x),E(t,x),B(t,x)]
is the smooth solution constructed in Proposition
1.1
for the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.1.2
);

•

Choose
T_{c}
suitably so that (
1.26
) is valid for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

•

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for
N\geq 4
,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\alpha_{0}+|\alpha|\leq N+4k-2n+2}

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial^{\alpha}[\rho_{n,0},u_{n,0},T_{n,0},E_{n,0},B_{n,0}](x)\right\|\leq C,\ \ 1\leq n\leq 2k-1;

(1.33)

•

Let
k\geq 4
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(0)\lesssim 1.

Then, there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
, the Cauchy problem of the VMB system (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) admits a unique smooth solution
[F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\varepsilon}(t,x),B^{\varepsilon}(t,x)]
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &F^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}F_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}F^{\varepsilon}_{R}\geq 0,\quad E^{\varepsilon}=E+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}E_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}E^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad B^{\varepsilon}=B+\sum_{n=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{n}B_{n}+\varepsilon^{k}B^{\varepsilon}_{R}.\end{aligned}

Furthermore, for
\kappa=\frac{1}{3}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(s)\,ds\lesssim\mathcal{E}(0)+1,\ 0\leq t\leq\varepsilon^{-\kappa},

(1.34)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\varepsilon^{-\kappa}}\Big(\left\|\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t)\left(F^{\varepsilon}(t)-\mathbf{M}(t)\right)\right\|_{H^{4}}+\left\|E^{\varepsilon}(t)-E(t)\right\|_{H^{4}}+\left\|B^{\varepsilon}(t)-B(t)\right\|_{H^{4}}\Big)=0.

Remark 1.2
.

A similar result also holds for the cutoff VMB system with
-3<\gamma\leq 1
. The only difference is that we replace the weight function
\overline{w}
with
w=\exp\big(\frac{\langle v\rangle^{2}}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\big)
.

1.5.
Strategies and ideas

In this subsection, we will outline the main ideas and strategies applied in our analysis.

A prevalent challenge encountered when validating the Hilbert expansion for non-relativistic kinetic equations within the realm of perturbation lies in a phenomenon known as “velocity growth”. It can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t,x,v)\Big\{\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\Big\}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(t,x,v)\sim|v|^{3}f^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v).

(1.35)

To overcome this difficulty, Caflisch
[
6
]
introduced a significant decomposition:

F^{\varepsilon}_{R}=\underbrace{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}}_{\text{low velocity part}}+\underbrace{\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}}_{\text{high velocity part}},

by partitioning
F^{\varepsilon}_{R}
in this manner, the equation corresponding to the angular cutoff Boltzmann equation was artificially separated into a coupled system involving
\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}
and
\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}
. Employing an energy-based approach,
\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}
and
\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}
were subsequently solved, with the assumption that
\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=0
. However, it’s worth pointing out that this assumption may not guarantee the non-negativity of the density distribution function
F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
.
To address this concern, Guo-Jang-Jiang
[
34
,
35
]
introduced the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
method, initially proposed in
[
29
]
for solving the initial boundary value problem of the Boltzmann equation. They utilized this method to justify the Hilbert expansion of the angular cutoff Boltzmann equation and established the non-negativity of the density distribution function
F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
. In their works
[
34
,
35
]
, the intricate term (
1.35
) was estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Big|\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big|\bar{f}^{\varepsilon},\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}\Big\rangle\lesssim\|\nabla_{t,x}(\rho,u,T)\|\|\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\|\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}\|.

(1.36)

Here,
\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle(1+|v|)^{-\beta}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}=F^{\varepsilon}_{R},\quad\beta>\frac{9-2\gamma}{2}.

We also emphasize that (
1.36
) holds under the constraint that
\frac{1}{2}\sup_{t,x}T(t,x)<T_{c}<\inf_{t,x}T(t,x)
for a positive constant
T_{c}
. Furthermore, it’s noteworthy that the estimates for
\|\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}
were established using a characteristic method, and the total energy was balanced through a clever interplay between norms
\|\bar{f}^{\varepsilon}\|
and
\|\bar{h}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}
.

This method found success in demonstrating the validity of the Hilbert expansion for various systems, including the relativistic Boltzmann equation
[
70
]
, the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system
[
33
]
, and the relativistic VMB system
[
37
]
.

It’s worth noting that the adapted
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
method developed in
[
37
]
faces limitations when applied to the non-relativistic VMB system. This limitation arises due to the absence of a corresponding Glassey-Strauss expression
[
23
]
for the electromagnetic field within the relativistic framework. Furthermore, since this method relies on the characteristic method and the structured nature of the linearized Boltzmann collision operator with cut-off potentials, it becomes challenging to adapt this method to tackle the same problem for Landau-type equations and non-cutoff Boltzmann-type equations.

In the relativistic framework, the troublesome term akin to (
1.35
) exhibits linear growth only in momentum
p
. Exploiting this gradual momentum growth, Ouyang-Wu-Xiao
[
63
]
recently introduced a weighted energy method that involves the local Maxwellian to investigate the Hilbert expansion of relativistic Landau-type equations. Their key strategy was to control the linear momentum growth term by incorporating an additional dissipation term induced by a special time-velocity dependent weight function. However, this method exclusively applies to relativistic kinetic equations, and addressing the case for non-relativistic Landau-type equations and non-cutoff Boltzmann-type equations remains an open problem.

This article centers on precisely this problem. Our primary objective is to establish the global validity of the Hilbert expansion for both the VML system and the non-cutoff VMB system, which serve as fundamental models for studying the dynamics of dilute charged particles.

Inspired by the works of
[
6
]
and
[
63
]
, we aspire to develop an enhanced energy method that accommodates both the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
and the global Maxwellian
\mu
. Our approach hinges on establishing the following pivotal relationship:

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}_{R}=\sqrt{\mathbf{M}}f^{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{\mu}h^{\varepsilon},\quad\text{with}\quad T_{c}<\inf_{t,x}T(t,x).

(1.37)

The crux of our analysis revolves around the interplay of energy estimates for both
f^{\varepsilon}
and
h^{\varepsilon}
. This harmonious interplay enables us to effectively close the energy estimates and, as a result, establish the well-posedness of the remainder
F_{R}^{\varepsilon}
within the framework of Sobolev space. It’s noteworthy that the global Maxwellian
\mu
adheres to the same functional form as presented in (
1.20
). The specific range of the positive constant
T_{c}>0
is meticulously specified in Corollary
1.1
, underscoring the precision of our approach.
To make our presentation more clear, we will present precise explanations for each case individually.

For the VML system, our main ideas are to introduce an interplay of energy estimates of
f^{\varepsilon}
and
h^{\varepsilon}
, which can be summarized as follows.

•

To effectively estimate
f^{\varepsilon}
and tackle the difficult term represented by (
1.35
), we adopt a strategy that involves dividing the velocity space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
into two distinct regions: the low-velocity portion and the high-velocity portion.
In the low-velocity region, we are able to provide bounds for both the microscopic and macroscopic components of
f^{\varepsilon}
. Specifically, we employ terms like
\varepsilon^{-1}\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})[f^{\varepsilon}]\|_{\bf D}^{2}
and
\sum_{|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq|\alpha|}\|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
to effectively control these components.
However, for the high-velocity portion, the following significant estimate was used

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\right|\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{|\alpha|}{\langle v\rangle}^{2(|\alpha|-j)}\exp\left(-\frac{(T-T_{c})|v|^{2}}{8C_{0}RTT_{c}}\right)\left|\nabla_{x}^{j}h^{\varepsilon}\right|,\quad 0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2.

This estimate directly follows from the assumption that
\mathbf{M}
and
\mu
are sufficiently close. To effectively control the high-velocity portion, we utilize norms like
\sum_{|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq|\alpha|}\|\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
. Similar techniques can also be applied to handle other nonlinear terms, such as
-(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}
, which involve velocity growth and/or velocity derivatives.
The dissipation norms
\|\partial^{\alpha}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{{\bf D}}
for
0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2
are specifically applied to control estimates related to
\nabla_{v}f^{\varepsilon}
, since the energy functional
\mathcal{E}(t)
for the VML system does not include any velocity derivatives.

•

For the estimates of
h^{\varepsilon}
, we encounter a problematic term
\varepsilon^{-1}\big|\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}],\partial^{\alpha}h^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle\big|
, which can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big|\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{d}[h^{\varepsilon}],\partial^{\alpha}h^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle\big|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{\varepsilon}\big\|h^{\varepsilon}\big\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{\bf D}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{\varepsilon}\Big(\big\|({\bf I}-{\bf P})[h^{\varepsilon}]\big\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{\bf D}}^{2}+\big\|{\bf P}[h^{\varepsilon}]\big\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{\bf D}}^{2}\Big).

(1.38)

However, controlling the macroscopic component
\epsilon_{1}\varepsilon^{-1}\big\|{\bf P}[h^{\varepsilon}]\big\|_{H^{|\alpha|}_{\bf D}}^{2}
presents difficulties. To address this issue, our paper introduces another approach to bound this troublesome term by using
f^{\varepsilon}
. It can be shown that

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf P}\left[h^{\varepsilon}\right]\right\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}_{\bf D}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|{\bf P}\left[f^{\varepsilon}\right]\right\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}_{\bf D}}\lesssim\left\|f^{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}},

(1.39)

which follows from (
1.37
) once again.

In addition to this main difficulty in estimating
h^{\varepsilon}
, we also face additional difficulties arising from the interaction between particles and the electromagnetic field. These include terms like
\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}
,
(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}
, etc., which involve velocity growth and derivatives and thus cannot be directly controlled in the energy estimates.

In fact, direct estimates of the delicate term
\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}
leads to temporal decay but velocity growth in the form of

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}(1+t)^{-p_{0}}\|\sqrt{{\langle v\rangle}}w_{0}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}

(1.40)

for some
p_{0}>1
. This growth cannot be controlled by either the energy functional
\mathcal{E}(t)
via Gronwall’s inequality or the dissipation terms induced by the linearized Landau collision operator. To tackle this issue, we introduce a time-velocity dependent exponential weight function
\exp\left(\frac{\langle v\rangle^{2}}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right)

which leads to the following additional dissipative term:

\frac{1}{8RT_{c}(\mathrm{e}+t)\big(\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)\big)^{2}}\|{\langle v\rangle}w_{0}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}.

With this additional term and the favorable temporal decay of solutions to the Euler-Maxwell system (
1.1.2
), we can then control (
1.40
) in a suitable manner. Note that this kind of time-velocity exponential weight was originally introduced in
[
17
,
18
,
8
]
to construct global smooth solutions near Maxwellians to some complex kinetic equations.

Regarding the term
(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}
, we employ a similar approach to that in
[
30
]
. We design a polynomial weight depending on the order of the
x-
derivatives to alleviate the velocity growth. However, this special design of the polynomial part also introduces new difficulties for the weighted trilinear estimates of the nonlinear collision operator
\varepsilon^{k-1}\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},h^{\varepsilon})
, where Sobolev’s inequalities like
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{2}_{x}}
have to be used, and additional polynomial weights may arise.
Our approach to solve this problem is to replace the additional polynomial weights with the exponential weight
\exp\left(\frac{\langle v\rangle^{2}}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\right)
, as shown in estimates (
2.3
)-(
2.58
) for more details.

•

To capture the interplay between the estimates of
f^{\varepsilon}
and
h^{\varepsilon}
, we introduce the energy functional
\mathcal{E}(t)
defined in (
1.27
). In fact, during our energy estimates for the function
f^{\varepsilon}
, as observed in
[
63
]
, we encounter a difficulty arising from the non-commutative structure of the linearized collision operator term
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]
and
{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}[f^{\varepsilon}]
with the spatial derivative operator
\partial^{\alpha}
. This leads to the appearance of singularities characterized by
\varepsilon^{-|\alpha|}
in the estimate of
\|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
. Similarly, when we derive an estimate for
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
as a consequence of (
1.38
) and (
1.39
), we encounter a term
\varepsilon^{-1}\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
with a singular factor of
\varepsilon^{-1}
in its upper bound estimates.

To remove these singularities and ensure that
\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}
remains integrable over the time interval
[0,\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}})
, which is the lifespan of our Hilbert expansion, we introduce coefficients
\varepsilon^{|\alpha|}
for the norm
\|\partial^{\alpha}f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
with
0\leq|\alpha|\leq 2
. Additionally, by multiplying the norms
\|\partial^{{\alpha}}h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}
by a weight factor of
\varepsilon^{|\alpha|+1+\kappa}
and
combining these energy estimates for
\|f^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{2}}
and
\|h^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{H^{2}}
, we are able to close the
a priori
estimates.

In the case of the non-cutoff VMB system, compared to the VML case, the corresponding problem becomes more intricate, and the main difficulties can be summarized in two aspects:

1.

The dissipation property of the non-cutoff linearized Boltzmann collision operator is weaker than that of the Landau case;

2.

In contrast to the Landau case, where we have weighted estimates of the form
e^{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}
(with
q>0
) for the Landau collision operators, the existence of similar estimates for the non-cutoff Boltzmann collision operators remains uncertain at present.

Our main strategies for addressing the aforementioned problems can be summarized as follows:

Firstly, we recognize the importance of introducing additional terms involving velocity derivatives into our energy functional, as expressed in (
1.4.2
). This step is crucial for effectively handling nonlinear terms such as
\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT}h^{\varepsilon}
and
-(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}
within the system.

Secondly, we leverage the estimates on the non-cutoff Boltzmann collision operators obtained in
[
12
,
21
]
to derive refined versions of these estimates with respect to the weight function
\overline{w}(t,v)=\exp\big(\frac{\langle v\rangle}{8RT_{c}\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\big)
, as detailed in Lemma
3.3
. With these refined estimates, we are able to handle the terms involving the interaction between particles and the electromagnetic field, the nonlinear collision terms, and the associated coefficients, as demonstrated in the following:

•

The interaction between particles and the electromagnetic field, exemplified by terms like
\frac{E\cdot v}{2RT_{c}}h^{\varepsilon}
and
(E+v\times B)\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varepsilon}
, can be effectively controlled through the additional dissipation terms induced by the weight function
\overline{w}(t,v)
. It’s worth noting that the weight function
\overline{w}(t,v)
lacks polynomial components and remains unchanged regardless of the order of derivatives. This distinctive feature simplifies our estimations and renders them relatively more concise.

•

To estimate the nonlinear terms related to
F_{1}
, such as

\big|\big\langle[\Gamma(\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{1},h^{\varepsilon})+\Gamma(h^{\varepsilon},\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{1})],\overline{w}^{2}h^{\varepsilon}\big\rangle\big|,

if we apply the estimates from

[
12
, Lemma 2.3]
,
such a term would be bounded by

[\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)]^{-2}\big\|\mu^{\frac{\lambda_{0}}{128}}F_{1}\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{v}}\left\|\overline{w}h^{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{1+\gamma/2},

(1.41)

which becomes unmanageable due to the linear time growth of
F_{1}
as indicated in (
4.3
).
To overcome this difficulty, we improved the estimate of
\left|\left\langle{\Gamma}\left(g_{1},g_{2}\right),\overline{w}^{2}g_{3}\right\rangle\right|
by employing a more precise angular integration estimate, resulting in the removal of undesirable terms like (
1.41
). For details, please refer to the proof of (
3.10
) in Lemma
3.3
. Indeed, the estimates in Lemma
3.3
are inherently interesting.

•

For the coefficients
F_{n}
(
1\leq n\leq 2k-1
), it is essential to establish velocity decay estimates of the form

\displaystyle\left|F_{n}\right|\lesssim\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-q\langle v\rangle},\qquad q>0.

(1.42)

In previous works such as
[
35
,
33
]
, similar estimates to (
1.42
) were derived directly from
e^{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}
(
q>0
) type weighted estimates on
\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n}
.
However, at the current stage, it is evident that
\mathbf{M}\mu^{-1}\sim e^{c_{1}\langle v\rangle^{2}}
for some
c_{1}>0
, even though
\mathbf{M}
and
\mu
are sufficiently close as our assumption (
1.26
). This implies that (
1.42
) cannot be derived through
e^{q\langle v\rangle}
(
q>0
) type weighted estimates on
\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n}
. To overcome this difficulty, our strategy is to use
e^{q\langle v\rangle}
(
q>0
) type weighted estimates on
\mathbf{\mu}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n}
instead of
\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}F_{n}
.

In other words, when constructing the coefficients
F_{n}
for
1\leq n\leq 2k-1
, we set
F_{n}=\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{n}=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h_{n}
. We then establish estimates like (
1.42
) using
h_{n}
. For more details, please refer to Lemma
4.2
.

1.6.
Literatures

Numerous studies have investigated the connections between kinetic equations and fluid dynamics systems, such as the Euler or Navier-Stokes equations. These relationships between the fluid-dynamic perspective and kinetic descriptions can be explored through various mathematical approaches. One such approach involves examining the equivalence of kinetic equations and fluid systems in a time-asymptotic sense. A lot of contributions to this area including works on Boltzmann-type equations like
[
51
,
10
,
42
,
43
,
56
,
59
]
, and for Landau-type equations, references like
[
13
,
14
]
have been made.

Another well-known strategy is to explore the small Knudsen number limits of kinetic equations. Typically, there are three distinct approaches to investigate such approximations:

•

One approach is based on the Chapman-Enskog procedure
[
7
,
22
]
, where the first-order approximation leads to the compressible Euler equations, and the second-order approximation results in the compressible Navier-Stokes system. In the context of the Boltzmann equation, the compressible Euler approximation was rigorously established in
[
62
,
71
]
within the framework of analytical function spaces, and in
[
73
,
72
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
near the Riemann solutions of the one-dimensional Euler equations. Regarding the global validity of the compressible Navier-Stokes approximation for Boltzmann-type equations, references
[
54
,
58
,
11
]
address this issue for the Boltzmann equation, and
[
16
]
considers the VMB system.

For the Landau equation, the validity of the Chapman-Enskog expansion was established in
[
15
]
.

•

The second program focuses on the incompressible limit. For Boltzmann-type equations, the incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit has been extensively studied in various works such as
[
4
,
5
,
24
,
25
,
49
,
57
,
61
,
66
,
3
]
in the framework of renormalized solutions, and
[
19
,
20
,
28
,
2
,
45
,
50
]
for classical solutions. Additionally, investigations into the incompressible Euler limit for Boltzmann-type equations can be found in works like
[
57
,
65
,
66
,
46
]
.

For the Landau equation, the incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier limit was explored in
[
28
,
64
]
.

•

The third program involves rigorously justifying the small Knudsen number limits of kinetic equations using the Hilbert expansion. For the Boltzmann equation, the validity of this expansion was established in
[
6
,
52
,
53
]
through a decomposition of the remainder equation. However, it was noted that these solutions constructed using this method did not guarantee non-negativity. This issue was resolved in
[
34
,
35
]
with the help of the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
approach developed in
[
29
]
. This approach has been successfully extended to address systems like the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system
[
33
]
, relativistic Boltzmann-type equations
[
70
,
37
]
, and more. For the initial boundary value problems associated with these equations, references like
[
32
,
47
,
48
]
provide further insights.

It’s important to note that the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
argument relies on the favorable properties of the linearized Boltzmann collision operator with cut-off potentials. Adapting this method to deal with the same problem for the VMB system with cut-off potentials, let alone the Landau-type equations and non-cutoff Boltzmann-type equations, appears challenging.

For the relativistic VML system, the problematic term analogous to (
1.35
) shows linear growth only in momentum
p
. The global validity of its Hilbert expansion was established in
[
63
]
using a weighted energy method based on a time-momentum related exponential weight function
\exp\Big\{\frac{c_{0}\sqrt{1+|p|^{2}}}{\ln(\mathrm{e}+t)}\Big\}
, where
c_{0}>0
. However, it’s important to note that this method is specific to relativistic kinetic equations. Addressing the same problem for non-relativistic Landau-type equations and non-cutoff Boltzmann-type equations remains an open problem. Considering that the VML and the non-cutoff VMB systems serve as fundamental models for the dynamics of dilute charged particles, this paper concentrates on investigating these two systems.

It should be pointed out that partial of our results about the VML system was announced in
[
55
]
.

1.7.
The structure

The remainder of our paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we present the proof of the validity of the global Hilbert expansion for the VML system. Section
3
is dedicated to demonstrating the validity of the global Hilbert expansion for the non-cutoff VMB system. In Section
4
, we provide the methodology for determining the coefficients
[F_{n},E_{n},B_{n}](1\leq n\leq 2k-1)
, particularly within the context of the non-cutoff VMB system.

2.
Global Hilbert expansion for the VML system

In this section, our focus is on justifying the validity of the Hilbert expansion (
1.4
) for the VML system (
1.1
). As discussed in the previous section, the proof relies on two main ingredients:

•

Determining the coefficients
[F_{n},E_{n},B_{n}]
for
1\leq n\leq 2k-1
. These coefficients are obtained through the iteration equations (
1.1.1
).

•

Proving the well-posedness of the remainder terms
[f^{\varepsilon},E^{\varepsilon}_{R},B^{\varepsilon}_{R}]
and
h^{\varepsilon}
using the equations (
1.16
) and (
1.2
) with prescribed initial data given by (
•
‣
2
) and (
2.2
):

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v)=\varepsilon^{-k}\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\{F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v)-\sum\limits_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{k}F_{n,0}(x,v)\right\},

\displaystyle E_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=

\displaystyle E^{\varepsilon}_{R,0}(x)=\varepsilon^{-k}\left\{E^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)-\sum\limits_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{k}E_{n,0}(x)\right\},

(2.1)

\displaystyle B_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0,x)=

\displaystyle B^{\varepsilon}_{R,0}(x)=\varepsilon^{-k}\left\{B^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)-\sum\limits_{n=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{k}B_{n,0}(x)\right\},

and

\displaystyle h^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=h^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v)=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{M}_{[\rho_{0},u_{0},T_{0}]}^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,v).

(2.2)

These ingredients combined with the iteration equations (
1.1.1
) will provide the necessary framework to establish the Hilbert expansion for the VML system.

2.1.
Preliminaries

In this subsection, we will compile some estimates for the Landau collision operators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{d}
as defined in (
1.18
), (
1.19
), and (
1.22
), respectively. Although estimates for the operators
\mathcal{L}
and
\Gamma
have been well-established in previous works such as
[
27
,
69
,
30
]
, it is not straightforward to derive corresponding estimates in the scenario of the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
. In this specific case, the formulation of Landau collision operators becomes more intricate, with additional terms involving the fluid velocity
u
. Apart from dealing with these additional terms, the main difficulty arises from the non-commutativity of the spatial derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}
and the Landau collision operators, as well as the projection operator
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{M}}
. However, due to the assumption of smallness in
u
and the favorable time decay of
[\rho,u,T]
in (
1.13
), the coercive property of the linearized collision operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
is maintained. Trilinear estimates for
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
, similar to those provided in
[
27
,
69
,
30
]
, can be derived through careful analysis and estimation.

Before delving into the estimates for the Landau collision operators, we will first provide explicit expressions for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{d}
,
\mathcal{K}_{d}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The expressions for the operators
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{d}
,
\mathcal{K}_{d}
, and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
are provided as follows:
For
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=&\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial_{j}f\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{(v_{i}-u_{i})(v_{j}-u_{j})}{4R^{2}T^{2}}f+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\Big]f,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}[f]=&-\mathbf{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\mathbf{M}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\Big)\Big)\Big].\end{array}\right.

For
\mathcal{A}_{d}
and
\mathcal{K}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathcal{A}_{d}[h]=&\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{i}}{2RT_{c}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h\\[5.69054pt]
&+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\partial_{j}h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{4R^{2}T_{c}T}f+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}\Big]h,\\[5.69054pt]
\mathcal{K}_{d}[h]=&\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\frac{u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{(T-T_{c})\mu-T_{c}(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big)\Big]\\[5.69054pt]
&-\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\big(\mathbf{M}-\mu\big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big)\Big].\end{array}\right.

For
\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}(f_{1},f_{2})=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{1}\Big)\partial_{j}f_{2}-\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{j}f_{1}\Big)f_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle-\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{1}\Big)\partial_{j}f_{2}+\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{j}f_{1}\Big)f_{2}.

Here,
\partial_{i}=\partial_{v_{i}}
for
i=1,2,3
, and

\sigma^{ij}_{g}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\phi^{ij}(v-v^{\prime})g(v^{\prime})dv^{\prime}.

Proof.

The proof will be carried out in a similar manner as in
[
27
, Lemma 1, p. 395]
. For brevity, we will only present (
2.1
).

To this end, a direct calculation yields,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{d}[h]=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{M}-\mu,\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)-\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\Big(\frac{-v_{j}+u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{v_{j}}{RT_{c}}\mu\Big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)\mathbf{M}\Big)\Big]+\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\Big(\partial_{j}h+\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{i}}{2RT_{c}}\Big(\frac{-u_{j}}{RT}+\frac{T_{c}-T}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)h

\displaystyle+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\partial_{j}h\Big]-\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{i}v_{j}}{4R^{2}T_{c}T}h+\partial_{i}\Big[\sigma^{ij}_{(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}\Big]h.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{d}[h]=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{C}(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h,\mathbf{M}-\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big)\Big(\frac{-v_{j}+u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{v_{j}}{RT_{c}}\mu\Big)

\displaystyle-\big(\mathbf{M}-\mu\big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\Big(\frac{u_{j}}{RT}\mathbf{M}+\frac{(T-T_{c})\mu-T_{c}(\mathbf{M}-\mu)}{RT_{c}T}v_{j}\Big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}h\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle-\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}\Big[\big(\mathbf{M}-\mu\big)\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}h-\frac{v_{j}}{2RT_{c}}h\Big)\Big)\Big].

This is exactly (
2.1
) and the proof of Lemma
2.1
is complete.
∎

2.1.1.
The linearized collision operators

The following lemma is quoted from
[
27
, Corollary 1, p. 399]
, which states the lower bound of
{\bf D}-
norm defined by (
1.23
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let the
{\bf D}
-norm be defined as in (
1.23
). Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|g|_{\bf D}^{2}\geq C\left\{\left|(1+|v|)^{-\frac{3}{2}}{\bf P}_{v}[\nabla_{v}g]\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left|(1+|v|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{v})[\nabla_{v}g]\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left|(1+|v|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}g\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right\},

where
{\bf P}_{v}
is the projection defined by

{\bf P}_{v}h_{j}=\sum{h_{k}v_{k}}\frac{v_{j}}{|v|^{2}},\ \ 1\leq j\leq 3,

for any vector-valued function
h(v)=[h_{1}(v),h_{2}(v),h_{3}(v)]
.

We will now provide coercivity estimates for the linearized operators
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
, along with an upper bound for the difference operator
\mathcal{L}_{d}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The linearized operators
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}
satisfy the following coercivity estimates:

(i)

For
\mathcal{L}
as given by (
1.18
), we have

\big\langle\mathcal{L}h,h\big\rangle\gtrsim|({\bf I}-{\bf P})h|_{\bf D}^{2}.

(2.7)

(ii)

Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{[\rho,u,T]},
where
[\rho,u,T]
is determined by Proposition
1.1
, then there exists a constant
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,f\big)\geq\delta\big|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}.

(2.8)

Moreover, for
\ell\geq 0
,
q\in[0,1)
, and
|\beta|>0
, we have

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}f\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}-\big(\eta+C\epsilon_{1}\big)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|=|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}

(2.9)

\displaystyle-C(\eta)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|<|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}_{\bf D}.

For
|\beta|=0
, we have

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}f\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}-C\epsilon_{1}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha}{\bf 1}_{|\alpha|\geq 1}\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}

(2.10)

\displaystyle-C(\eta)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\big|\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{C(\eta)})}.

Here
\eta>0
is some small constant,
B_{C(\eta)}
is the ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with center
0
and radius
C(\eta)
.

In addition, for
|\alpha|>0
, there exists a polynomial
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)
with
\mathcal{U}(0)=0
such that

\displaystyle\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{3\delta}{4}\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{\bf D}^{2}-C\mathcal{U}(\|\nabla_{x}[\rho,u,T]\|_{W^{|\alpha|-1,\infty}})

(2.11)

\displaystyle\times\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{H^{|\alpha|-1}_{\bf D}}^{2}+\varepsilon\|f\|^{2}_{H^{|\alpha|}}\Big).

Proof.

(
2.7
) is directly taken from
[
27
, Lemma 5, p. 400]
. Since
[\rho,u,T]
satisfies (
1.13
), (
2.8
) follows once more from a similar argument as presented in the proof of
[
27
, Lemma 5, p. 400]
.

We will now prove (
(ii)
). Recall that
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}+\mathcal{K_{\mathbf{M}}}
, where
\mathcal{A_{\mathbf{M}}}
and
\mathcal{K_{\mathbf{M}}}
are defined by (
2.1
). Direct calculations yield the following expression:

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\tilde{{\bf D}}}^{2}

\displaystyle-{\bf 1}_{\alpha+\beta>0}\sum\limits_{0<\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\leq\alpha+\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big)

\displaystyle-\sum\limits_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{i}\left(\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\right)\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big)

\displaystyle-{\bf 1}_{\alpha+\beta>0}\sum\limits_{0<\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\leq\alpha+\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{(v_{i}-u_{i})(v_{j}-u_{j})}{4R^{2}T^{2}}\right)\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}\leq\alpha+\beta}C_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}C_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big(\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\left(\sigma^{ij}_{\mathbf{M}}\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}\right)\partial^{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}\partial_{j}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\partial_{i}f\big).

(2.12)

Here, we define:

\displaystyle\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\tilde{{\bf D}}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\sigma_{\mathbf{M}}^{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\partial_{v_{j}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\,dv

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4R^{2}T^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\sigma_{\mathbf{M}}^{ij}(v_{i}-u_{i})(v_{j}-u_{j})|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f|^{2}\,dv.

Considering that
[\rho,u,T]
satisfies (
1.13
), we can establish the following inequality:

\displaystyle\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\tilde{{\bf D}}}^{2}\gtrsim

\displaystyle\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}-C\epsilon_{1}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}.

Subsequently, by employing (
1.13
) and conducting calculations analogous to those found in the proof of
[
69
, Lemma 8, p. 319]
, we observe that the remaining terms on the R.H.S. of (
2.12
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\big(\eta+C\epsilon_{1}\big)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|=|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|_{\bf D}^{2}+C(\eta)\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha}\sum_{|\beta^{\prime}|<|\beta|}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta^{\prime}|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\beta^{\prime}}f\big|^{2}_{\bf D},

where
0<\eta\ll 1.

Moving on to estimate
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
, we start with (
2.1
), from which we derive the following expression:

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\partial_{i}\left[\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}f\Big)\Big)\right\}\right],\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\partial_{i}\left[\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\right]\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}f\Big)\Big)\right\},\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\phi^{ij}\ast\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\partial_{j}f+\frac{v_{j}-u_{j}}{2RT}f\Big)\Big)\right\},\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\right).

As for any finite
l\in\mathbb{N}
and any multi-indices
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},
it can be deduced from (
1.13
) that

\Big|\nabla_{v}^{l}\left\{\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\right\}\mathbf{M}^{1/2}\Big|\leq C\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1-q}{4}},

and

\Big|\partial_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\left\{\frac{v_{i}-u_{i}}{2RT}\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\Big|\leq C\mathbf{M}^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Using the same reasoning that was employed to derive estimates for
{\bf K}
in
[
69
, Lemma 8, p. 319]
, we can express it as

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\langle v\rangle^{2(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\{\eta\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\bf D}+C(\eta)\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}f\big|_{L^{2}(B_{C(\eta)})}\right\}\big|\langle v\rangle^{(\ell-|\beta|)}\mathbf{M}^{-q/2}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big|_{\bf D}.

When combining the above estimates for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{M}}
, we can conclude that (
(ii)
) holds true. (
(ii)
) can be proven similarly as described above.

Regarding (
2.11
), after applying (
2.8
), we can derive the following:

\displaystyle\big\langle\partial^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}f,\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f+\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\big\rangle+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{\bf D}^{2}+\big\langle\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\big\rangle

\displaystyle+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\delta\|\partial^{\alpha}({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f\|_{\bf D}^{2}-C\|\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},{{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}}}\Rbrack f\|_{\bf D}^{2}+\big\langle\Lbrack\partial^{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{M}}\Rbrack({\bf I}-{\bf P}_{\mathbf{M}})f,\partial^{\alpha}f\big\rangle.

Here,
\Lbrack A,B\Rbrack=AB-BA
denotes the commutator of operators
A
and
B
.

Noting that if
|\alpha|>0
,
\partial^{\alpha}
acts on the local Maxwellian
\mathbf{M}
at least once in the commutators
